The National Center for Agricultural and Forestry Technology 'Enrique Álvarez Córdova' (CENTA), is the official institution in charge of generating, disseminating and facilitating research and technology transfer, with the primary objective of contributing to the increase in production and productivity of the agro-livestock and forestry sector in El Salvador, in a friendly manner to the environment.

This TECHNICAL GUIDE TO LOROCO CULTIVATION has been prepared in a practical and simple way for easy understanding by producers, producers, and other actors related to the agro-livestock and forestry sector. The document provides basic information on agronomic management in order to be used as a support tool in decision-making in the production process.

This publication collects the knowledge acquired by specialist technicians as a result of the research carried out, bibliographic consultations and the exchange of knowledge with producers and producers and homologous institutions.

The objective of CENTA is that this technical guide constitutes a tool for research, learning and adoption of technologies; as a contribution to the technological development process with an agroecological approach, contributing to improving the food sovereignty and security of the country.

Ing. Santos Rafael Alemán Executive Director CENTA

## I. GENERAL INFORMATION

## IMPORTANCE

The loroco (Fernaldia pandurata Woodson) is a traditional crop that, until a few years ago, was only found in the wild or cultivated in home gardens by housewives and small farmers, without an appropriate handling technique, nor considering its nutritional value (Appendix 1), commercial value, and its multiple uses.

Nowadays, many farmers and exporters have identified the potential of this crop in both fresh and processed forms, and its possibility not only for a domestic market, but also for commercialization for export, primarily to the United States, Canada, and Italy, where Central American people live, including thousands of Salvadorans who are the largest consumers of this product, considered "nostalgic". El Salvador has had the admissibility or authorization of the United States to import fresh loroco since July 25, 2003, but this opportunity has not been fully exploited due to the high costs of air transportation and the short shelf life of the product under appropriate conditions, which makes distribution difficult.

Currently, the policies of the agricultural sector are focused on promoting agricultural diversification in the country and the export of non-traditional crops, as they represent new alternatives for generating revenue, and in this context, the loroco plays a very important role. The production of loroco is estimated at 90% during the rainy season and only 10% during the dry season. Even with irrigation systems, as it is a type of crop that responds to sunlight. According to data provided by the General Directorate of Agricultural Economy, the average price of fresh loroco in 2013 was $2.83 per pound for the domestic market, although it should be noted that during the dry season, prices can reach up to $10.00 per pound.

## ORIGIN AND DISTRIBUTION

This plant has a strong root in our culture, as our ancestors already knew it as 'Quilite', which is also the name currently used.

Technical Guide

in some places in the country. This word means in the Nahuatl language "cohollo", and in Spanish "edible herb". (CENTA, 2002)

It is known that the loroco originated in Central America and Mexico; the loroco flower is one of the main condiments in Salvadoran cuisine, being used to season soups, pizzas, and to prepare meat sauces, and it is also an ingredient in traditional pupusas.

In pre-Columbian times, the Maya civilization was the first to develop agriculture, discovering many products for its subsistence, including the loroco. This product was cultivated in family gardens, where it was used for edible purposes, without ever commercializing it. (Rubio Imbers, 2004)

The main areas where the highest concentration of this crop is found are:

## II. BOTANICAL ASPECTS

## ROOT

The loroco root is fibrous and deep, so it withstands the heat that occurs in the country. It contains substances with certain characteristics, namely alkaloids known as locina and loroquina, which are active principles that influence blood pressure. This plant develops rhizomes when it is approximately 6 months old, and these are the ones that give rise to new shoots when the rainy season begins.

## STEM

The stem is a thin (liana-type) weak, pubescent stem, about 1 cm thick, with fissures and many lenticels. The base is woody with branches that die after flowering in the wild or when there is no irrigation; it remains green inside during the dry season, if it is irrigated (Figure 1).

Figure 1. Loroco stem forming a liana

<!-- image -->

## LEAVES

The leaves are oblong, elliptical, opposite, quite acuminate, with the outer edges slightly wavy. With dimensions of 4 to 22 centimeters in length and 1.5 to 12 centimeters in width. The petiole is generally smooth and the underside may be pubescent (Figure 2). There are different leaf shapes depending on the variety.

It has been observed that plants have the same leaf shape throughout the cycle, but there are also plants in which a single plant has different leaf shapes as it develops.

Figures 2. Different shapes and sizes of loroco leaves

<!-- image -->

## FLOWER

It is the part that is harvested for human consumption; its consumption is varied, even in the form of tea, which is obtained by drying. The inflorescence occurs in clusters, and each one has between 10 and 32 flowers, resulting in an average of 25 per cluster. The corolla inside has many fine hairs that are visible when the flower is fresh.

The time of year when the plant produces flowers is from May to November, although if there is irrigation, it produces flowers for 10 months of the year. Generally, the plant enters a physiological rest from November to March. You can collect 30 to 40 clusters per plant every 3 days during its peak flowering period. Each cluster weighs approximately one gram (Figure 3).

Figure 3. Loroco flower.

<!-- image -->

Table 2. Iron and Zinc Content in 100 grams of Loroco flower.

## FRUIT

The fruit is composed of one, two, or more follicles, which are attached to a peduncle. This follicle can have different shapes: cylindrical, straight, or curved inward; these can reach a length of up to 34 cm, and a diameter of 5 to 6 mm (Figure 4).

Figure 4. Simple Loroco fruit.

<!-- image -->

When the fruit is young, it is green, and it turns dark brown as it ripens. The follicle is dehiscent (it opens only when ripe). Inside each follicle, there can be between 25 and 150 seeds, depending on its length. Its obtaining is difficult because the flower is constantly harvested for consumption.

## SEED

The Loroco seed has a length of 1.4 to 1.6 cm and a diameter between 2 and 3 mm, with a large number of hairs (cottony hairs) at the end, which facilitate its dispersion by wind (Figure 5). The seed has a high viability, and the germination percentage can reach 90%; after six months, this percentage can almost disappear. It is necessary to keep the seeds refrigerated in glass jars when collecting them to maintain their viability. The time it takes to germinate is 10 to 15 days, although in areas with temperatures above 30ºC, it can be 5 to 8 days.

## SEED COLLECTION

The optimal time to collect the seed is when the follicle begins to open. Its collection can be done in the field, tying bags around the peduncles of the follicles that are about to ripen. When removing them, the seeds will be inside the bags. Another way is to collect the follicles when they have reached their physiological maturity and extract the seeds manually; this practice is the most used in our country.

The climatic zone of El Salvador, where the Loroco grows, is called the hot tropical savannah or hot lands.

Figure 5. Loroco seed.

<!-- image -->

## III. CLIMATIC AND EDAPHIC REQUIREMENTS

## PRECIPITATION

The cultivation of Loroco develops best with an annual rainfall that oscillates between 1200 to 1800 mm

## TEMPERATURE

The average temperature at which the cultivation develops oscillates between 20 to 32°C; temperatures higher or lower than these ranges cause stress to the plant, which affects the production of flowers.

## RELATIVE HUMIDITY

The best relative humidity range oscillates between 70 to 77%.

## SOIL

It develops in various types of soils, from sandy to sandy-loamy. In soils with drainage problems, the physiological development of the plant is affected by the lack of oxygen in the soil, which favors the conditions for the development of root diseases, causing the leaves to turn yellow and fall off.

## ALTITUDE

The Loroco develops at a wide range of altitudes (20 to 1200 msnm), with the largest cultivated areas being between 20 and 800 msnm.

## IV. VARIETIES

The varieties observed in the field are those of our country. It is known that there are about 8 Creole varieties, which differ from each other in terms of characteristics such as: shape, color, width and length of the leaves, growth habit, branching, plant height, color and size of the flowers, flowering time and production. Currently, the varieties are not characterized.

## V. PROPAGATION

The Loroco is propagated in two ways: asexual and sexual. The asexual method includes the use of rhizomes and cuttings (vegetative material) that must come from physiologically mature, healthy, and productive plants, and the sexual method, by seed, which is obtained from ripe fruits.

Of the methods, the most used is the propagation by seed, due to its ease of handling.

## ASEXUAL PROPAGATION

## RHIZOME

When the Loroco plant is between 6 and 8 months old, it develops rhizomes or tubers in the roots, which at the beginning of the rains produce new shoots or sprouts. These sprouts, when collected, can be divided and placed in bags or directly in the field.

If planted in bags, they should be placed at a depth of 2 - 5 cm, where they will remain for a period of three months before being transplanted to the field.

## STEM CUTTINGS

The stem cuttings to be reproduced will be selected from the intermediate growth of the plant, with good health and production. This material should be cut between 2 and 3 centimeters below a node, with a length of 25 cm, containing at least 3 internodes; to avoid dehydration, the leaves are removed, and the mixture of 50% black soil plus 50% sand provides the best percentage of adhesion; the use of rooters is generally recommended at the end of the rainy season, because the vegetative material has more stored nutrients and facilitates the process.

## SEXUAL PROPAGATION

The propagation of loroco seeds can be done in three ways:

- a. Sowing in beds or trays (seedling bed)
- b. Direct sowing in pots
- c. Sowing in trays
- a. Sowing in beds

To obtain good germination of the seeds and good development of the seedlings, it is essential to provide them with good conditions. Therefore, it is recommended to sow the seeds in beds or trays, especially prepared with the best available material to help with germination, which should be light, fluffy, organic, with a low percentage of clay, free of salts, with good drainage and properly disinfected, to ensure good health of the same.

## VI. PREPARATION OF SOIL OR SUBSTRATE FOR BEDS

There is a wide variety of materials used for soil preparation, either alone or mixed, including: sand, compost, moss, peat, different sources of organic matter such as well-decomposed cow manure and worm humus.

Their use will depend on their availability in each area and their prices.

In the country, a fairly common mixture is river sand, soil and organic matter in a ratio of 2:2:1 respectively. In some places, sand is replaced with gravel.

## DISINFECTION OF SOIL OR SUBSTRATE OF BEDS

This is done with the aim of eliminating pathogens and weeds present in the soil. Disinfection can be done by physical and/or chemical means.

## · SOLARIZATION

This consists of taking advantage of the high temperatures produced by solar radiation. It is achieved by covering the moistened soil with a white or transparent plastic sheet of 1-1.5 mm thickness and sealing it afterwards with soil on all sides. It is left for a period of 30 to 40 days in places with high temperatures and solar radiation, and up to 60 days, in areas with lower temperatures. This process achieves the sterilization of the soil, making effective control of pests and favors the decomposition of organic matter, which results in a greater availability of nutrients for the plants.

## · USE OF BOILING WATER

This method of disinfection has been used for a long time by small farmers; in addition to being efficient, it is easy and economical. It consists of applying 1.5 gallons of boiling water per square meter of soil, using a watering can, leaving it to cool for 24 hours before sowing, if the soil is very dry, moisten it with warm water beforehand.

## DISINFECTION BY CHEMICAL MEANS

There are a number of products available on the market that can be used as soil disinfectants, insecticides and nematocides can be combined with fungicides, as well as fumigants.

## · USE OF INSECTICIDES AND FUNGICIDES

Granular insecticide can be used to control soil pests and sucking insects. Imidaclopid 0.8 GR is used, which provides 60 days of protection, with a recommended application rate of 16 kg.ha -1, another option is Triadimenol +Imidacloprid 1.4 GR (insecticide and fungicide) in a dose of 16 to 20 kg.ha -1.

When disinfection is with fumigants, the following steps should be followed regarding their application:

- Sprinkle the product on the soil according to the recommended dose for each product, using gloves to avoid direct contact with the hands.
- Mix it with the soil to a depth of 20 cm using spades or rakes.
- Moisten the soil completely.
- Cover with plastic and seal hermetically, keep it covered for the recommended time for each product.
- Remove the plastic, ventilate and turn over the soil to release any gases that may affect seed germination.

Other disinfection products:

## CAL

It is preferable to apply dolomite lime 5-8 days before sowing, incorporating it into the soil. The recommended dose is one pound per square meter.

It is used as a soil conditioner or fertilizer in general for all crops in soils with acidity, calcium, magnesium or indirectly potassium problems.

## ASH

It is incorporated into the soil or substrate three days before sowing, using two pounds per square meter. Ash provides good amounts of potassium, the macronutrient most demanded by plants after nitrogen. Potassium favors the growth of foliage and fruits and improves the plant's tolerance to lack of water. Ash also neutralizes the acidity of the soil and also has the characteristic of stimulating the activity of nitrogen-fixing bacteria in the soil.

## VII. CONSTRUCTION OF BEDS OR TRAYS

For the construction of beds, a flat and close to a water source is chosen. Once disinfected, the soil of the beds is removed, loosened and leveled. The profile of the bed or tray should be marked, using cords and stakes of 0.75 m long to accommodate the soil according to the selected measurements. The recommended width is between 0.8 and 1 m, the length will depend on the number of plants to be produced, the height will be between 0.20 - 0.25 m to ensure that the seedlings have good root development.

To better hold the soil and prevent washing, it is recommended to protect the sides of the bed with the material available in the area (bamboo, wood, brick or other). Approximately 700 seedlings can be obtained per square meter of loroco seedling bed, but a 10% loss should be taken into account.

## VIII. SOWING AND MANAGEMENT OF THE SEEDLING

Prior to sowing, it is advisable to lightly moisten the surface of the beds, in order to achieve good germination.

Fertilization should be carried out before sowing with a 15-15-15 formula at a rate of 100 grams per square meter.

Furrows are made on the seedling beds, spaced 12-15 cm apart, then the seeds are sown at a distance of 1 cm apart and a depth of 0.5 cm, covering lightly with soil.

After sowing, the seedling bed should be protected with suitable material such as rice husk, straw or any other material available in the place, making sure that it does not carry seeds or weeds, diseases or insects that could contaminate the seedling bed. This covering helps to prevent watering or rain from uncovering the seeds.

To make the seedling bed, beds of 1.2 m wide and 0.20 m high should be made, with a length that depends on the number of plants and the area of the land.

A good watering should be applied after sowing, subsequently, this activity should continue to ensure good plant development.

Any of the coverings used must be removed immediately when the emergency begins, which is between 5 and 8 days in areas with high temperatures and up to 14 days in areas with lower temperatures.

The seedlings must be inspected periodically to determine if there is any presence of pests and to be able to carry out timely control. It is advisable to protect the seedlings in their surroundings with mesh, plastic or covers, to prevent damage from animals such as poultry, pigs, reptiles and rabbits, among others. The seedlings to be transplanted will remain in the seedling bed for up to 30 days or when they reach between 8 and 10 cm in length and have two true leaves (Figure 6).

<!-- image -->

## BAG TRANSPLANT

The size of the bags to be used is 4 x 6 or 6 x 9 inches. These are filled with a mixture prepared of soil, organic matter and sand, in a ratio of 2:2:1, it is also possible to use materials such as gravel, rice husk or other available.

The transplant is carried out in cool hours to avoid dehydration and loss of seedlings. The seedlings transplanted in bags require watering to meet the water needs during the period they remain in the nursery.

<!-- image -->

Figure 6. Loroco nursery. Source: OIRSA, 2002

To carry out the watering, a micro-sprinkler can be used, which has the advantage of reducing labor costs, but it requires a greater initial investment. In many loroco nurseries, watering cans are used, which is a practice that does not require a lot of investment, but requires more labor.

In areas where the temperatures are greater than 30°C, it is advisable to provide the plant with some degree of shade through trellises, trees or mesh. In less hot places, the use of shade is not necessary.

The bags that contain the loroco plants, as well as their surroundings, must be kept free of pests, for which they must be inspected continuously.

Prior to the transplant in bag and during the nursery stage, it is recommended to apply a foliar fertilizer with multiple minerals in each stage, at doses according to technical instructions, and two applications with the formula 15-15-15 at a dose of 3.5 grams per plant

Technical Guide

for each application. This is to maintain the plants in good conditions of fertility.

These plants will remain in the nursery stage for a period of two months before being transplanted to the field.

Another practice is the sowing of loroco seeds in trays with 128 cells with a depth of two inches. When the plant is one month old, it is transplanted into bags, remaining in them for two months, before being sown in the final location.

The advantage obtained is the production of seedlings with good development and easy handling for their transplant. The care that must be taken should be similar to the recommendations in traditional nurseries and nurseries.

## B. DIRECT SOWING IN BAGS

Fill the bags with the mixture or substrate mentioned before, placing two seeds per bag at a depth of half a centimeter, then protect them with a thin layer of dry hay, free of weed seeds, in order to retain moisture and promote the germination of the seed.

Once the seedlings have emerged, the weeds must be removed continuously and the necessary management must be carried out as described above, in order to achieve their optimal development; the ideal is that the seedlings remain in the nursery for a maximum of three months.

## C. SOWING IN TRAYS

The sowing of loroco seeds in trays is a new practice in the country. It is carried out using trays with 128 cells, with a depth of two inches. When the plant is one month old, it is transplanted into bags, remaining in them for two months, before being sown in the final location. The advantage obtained is the production of seedlings with good development and easy handling for their transplant. The care that must be taken should be similar to the recommendations in traditional nurseries and nurseries.

It is very important to know the cultivation phenology to define each stage and to program to obtain better prices in the market (Table 3).

Table 3. Loroco cultivation phenology.

## X. CULTURAL LABORS

## SOIL PREPARATION

The good preparation of the soil depends largely on the success of the crop establishment, since this contributes, among other things, to that the roots of the plants develop well, provides good aeration and better utilizes nutrients.

If the place to be cultivated has previous crop residues, the preparation must be carried out in advance, in order to allow a good incorporation and decomposition of the vegetable waste. This cultural practice also allows for the control of weeds and some soil pests. The land must be prepared in the best way, it must be loose, leveled and with an adequate depth to facilitate drainage.

Prior to the soil preparation, it is necessary to carry out a chemical analysis of it, to determine its productive potential and the nutritional needs that must be provided to the crop. Take samples of soil for its respective pest analysis (phytopathological, entomological and nematological) and thus, determine if there is a need to carry out a control.

The soil preparation labors are different from one place to another, and depend on factors such as: the topography of the land, the presence of a plow, types of weeds, moisture content and the farmer's economic capacity, since in some cases the preparation only consists of cleaning and hoeing the land.

Loroco can be cultivated in slopes, flat soils and mechanized soils. In sloping soils, minimum tillage is implemented, making level curves with a slope of no more than 2% to better utilize water and facilitate all the cultivation tasks. It is necessary to implement soil conservation measures such as: construction of slopes, terraces and the use of live barriers, to prevent or reduce the washing of the land.

The soil preparation in flat soils comprises: subsoiling, if there are compaction problems. This is achieved by passing a subsoiler at a depth of 40 - 60 cm and one or two passes of a rake, until the soil is well loosened and leveled.

## XI. SOWING

## SOWING IN THE FINAL LOCATION

This labor comprises all the activities aimed at establishing the plants in the final location, where the best possible conditions are provided for their good development.

## STAKING AND STAKING

It is necessary to have the quantity of stakes according to the area to be cultivated and the planting density. These stakes must have a length between 40-50 cm.

With staking and staking, it is possible to have the appropriate spatial distribution of the plants in the land.

## SUBSOILING

The depth of the planting hole will be according to the physical conditions of the soil; in soils with a coarse texture, it is recommended to make holes of 25 x 25 x 25 cm. In soils with a franco-clay and franco-sandy texture, it is recommended to make holes of 30 x 30 x 30 cm; and in soils with a higher clay content, it is necessary to make holes of 40 x 40 x 40 cm.

With these planting hole dimensions, the plants will be provided with good conditions for their root development. The ideal is to place a layer of organic matter at the bottom of the hole and the soil with which the root system will be covered should be mixed with organic matter and with the chemical fertilizer. Add (58 g of formula 16-20-0 or 15-15-15) as needed according to the soil chemical analysis.

## TRANSPLANTING SEASON

The best time to transplant for loroco production is at the beginning of the rainy season; which comprises the months of May and June, taking into account that most farmers do not have irrigation in their fields. In areas with irrigation, it can be transplanted at any time of the year, however, the months of September to October are recommended to obtain production in January and February, where the prices are the highest, reaching the best sales prices and maintaining them until March and April, when the lowest prices are observed when the rainy season is in full swing (Figure 7).

Figure 7. Average monthly price trend in US dollars of 30 lb loroco from 2012 to 2016.

<!-- image -->

Source: Statistical yearbooks, MAG. El Salvador.

Observing the monthly price history of the last 4 years, it manifests that the best price for the 30 lb loroco is from January with an average of $102.25 to March with $123.95, from April to October the price decreases, observing its lowest price in October with $24.39, the average annual is $61.70 (Table 3).

XII. PRODUCTION SYSTEMS

## MONOCULTURE

This consists of establishing the crop without competition from other crops, at shorter spacings than those that allow for a greater number of plants per unit of area (Figure 8).

Figure 8. Monoculture loroco system.

<!-- image -->

## ASSOCIATED CULTIVATION

This consists of sowing loroco associated with another crop such as: pipián, cucumber, tomato, chili, camote, corn, bean, chipilín, mora, papaya or cover legumes. The spacings used are larger compared to those used in monoculture (Figure 9).

Figure 9. Associated cultivation system in loroco.

<!-- image -->

It is important to mention that loroco also develops in the wild, appearing and developing in association with other plants such as coffee, citrus and corn.

## POLYCULTURE

This consists of establishing loroco as the main crop and different crops can be sown in the rows. It is possible to create blocks of cover crops, horticultural crops or insect repellent plants. This system allows for diversification of production, more efficient use of the soil resource, as well as repelling pests and controlling weeds (Figure 10).

Figure 10. Polyculture in loroco.

<!-- image -->

## PLANTING DENSITY

The number of plants per unit of area will be determined according to the production system that you want to establish and the quality of the available soil (Table 4).

Table 4. Spacings in loroco cultivation.

## XIII. FERTILIZATION

For the application of fertilizers, it is recommended to make a soil sampling beforehand, which will be the most appropriate basis to meet the nutrient demands of the plant.

In the case of lacking the results of the sampling, as a general recommendation, a yearly dosage for loroco cultivation is used of N=300 kg, P= 100 kg, K= 100 kg per hectare (Zamora López, 2014)

The NPK sources to be used will be formula 15-15-15, ammonium sulfate and urea, for this, the following amounts can be used throughout the crop cycle (Table 5).

To satisfy the needs of the loroco crop per hectare, apply the amount of fertilizer indicated in table 6 per plant.

The advantages that this fertilization provides are:

- ·Greater number of cuts per harvest period, improves the size and coloration of the flower.
- ·Increase of 30-40% in total production per area.
- ·Maintains the nutritional balance of the soils.

The recommended domains for loroco cultivation:

For small and medium loroco producers in the coastal and central region of the country, with altitudes ranging from 60-1,200 msnm, semi-flat and sloping topographies, with temperatures ranging from 20-32ºc, this crop develops very well in well-drained soils, from sandy to clayey soils with a pH of 5.5-6.5 (Zamora López, 2014).

## XIV. CULTIVATION MANAGEMENT

When the loroco plant is placed in its final location, it requires a series of activities that facilitate its development, such as pruning, training, and pest control.

## PRUNING

This work is done to stimulate the plant to produce new shoots and increase production. It is generally carried out annually, at the end of August and the beginning of September, when there is irrigation in the plot; in addition, it is to separate the period when the price starts to fall due to the oversupply of product.

It is recommended to prune only the main guide, removing the lateral shoots, observing harvesting from November to February, and higher yields, up to 0.69 t.ha -1 (Ramirez, CA. 2003).

After carrying out the pruning, oxichloride of copper should be applied to prevent diseases in the cuts made and a flowering inducer should be applied.

## SUPPORT SYSTEM

Due to its growth habits (trellis), it is necessary to establish structures that serve as support for the plant to promote aeration, light reception, and therefore growth and increased flower production.

There are several support structures, but the most used are: the trellis and the vertical espaldera.

## TRELLIS

This consists of placing posts in the plot, which on the upper part support rows of galvanized wire # 14 along the entire area of the plot and over which the guides (Figure 11) will develop.

The recommended spacing between posts for this system is between 2.5 m to 3 m in the plot, the length of the support is 2.5 m, at least 0.1 m in diameter, and it must be buried in the soil 0.5 m. They can be made of bamboo (Phyllostachys aurea), tigüilote (Cordia dentata Poir), madrecacao (Gliricidia sepium), capulín (Muntingia calabura L), or chaquiro (Podocarpus oleifolius), among others; these must be physiologically mature and preferably treated against the caterpillars for greater durability. Cement posts can also be used. The supports at the ends of the furrow must be thicker and must be anchored to better support the weight.

## ADVANTAGES:

- Lower incidence of weeds.

## DISADVANTAGES:

- Makes it difficult to carry out phytosanitary and harvesting work.
- Does not allow for associations with other crops.
- Difficulty in pruning.

## VERTICAL ESPALDERA

This consists of installing vertical posts on the planting line, which support 3 to 4 rows of wire, placed in a horizontal and parallel position, at a distance of 0.40 m, similar to a fence. The distance between the posts on the line should be twice the distance between plants.

The galvanized wire that is used must be #14 or #18, depending on the dimensions of the espaldera. It is recommended that the espalderas have a north-south orientation, which allows for a more uniform distribution of sunlight on the plants. The recommended length of the espaldera should be equivalent to 10 consecutive plants, for example, when the distance between plants is 2 m, the distance between posts should be 4 m and the length of the espaldera 20 m (Figure 12).

Figure 11. View of loroco trellis. Second year of cultivation.

<!-- image -->

## ADVANTAGES:

- Less material is used compared to the trellis.
- Easy application of pesticides.
- Reduces the possibility of poisoning for people who apply pesticides.
- Facilitates all the work of the crop (irrigation, fertilization, pruning, training, and other).
- Facilitates harvesting.

## DISADVANTAGES:

- If not using sufficiently strong posts and at an appropriate distance, the wind can blow the espaldera.
- If the guides are not managed properly, an entanglement is formed that reduces production and affects the quality of the flower.

Figure 12. Planting of vertical espaldera.

<!-- image -->

## XV. PEST CONTROL

## WEEDS

The elimination of weeds is of utmost importance because they compete for water, light, nutrients, and physical space with the crop, reducing production and the quality of the flowers.

The competition occurs throughout the vegetative cycle of the loroco, but it intensifies in the rainy months, because this is a good time to eliminate them, since in this period the plant needs to better utilize the nutrients and water for its flowering.

To reduce the damage and the costs incurred in the control of weeds, an efficient management plan should be carried out from the establishment of the nurseries.

This plan integrates the different field operations related to manual cleaning, mechanical control, and chemical control.

## · MECHANICAL CONTROL

This can be done with manual tools (cumas or azadones) or mechanized tools such as a chapodadora.

## · CHEMICAL CONTROL

There are two types of herbicides that can be used: the non-selective and the selective.

## Table 7. Herbicides that can be used in the loroco crop.

## Non-selective herbicides:

These have a wide spectrum of control, can destroy weeds and the crop, therefore they are recommended before planting and/or transplanting to control weeds that have emerged and exceed 2 cm in height. For the emergence of weeds after transplanting, i.e. when the crop is already established, the products aimed at the foliage of the weeds that are found in the rows can be applied, combining them with manual weeding around the loroco plants.

When the used growing system is the hedged system, the total development of the plantation reduces the incidence of weeds, in this case the manual or machete or scythe method is used.

## Selective herbicides:

These are inactive against broad-leaved crops, therefore they can be applied in any growth stage of the loroco without phytotoxicity problems, but always with care in their handling. In Table 6, some herbicides to be used are described.

## INSECTS

Within the main insects that damage the loroco crop in El Salvador we have:

## BLIND GOAT ( Phyllophaga spp.) (LARVA) OR CHICK (ADULT)

Within the soil insects, it is considered the most economically problematic.

The larvae of Phyllophaga destroy the roots of a wide range of grasses and other vegetables. They feed on roots and decaying organic matter (Figure 13).

Depending on the extent of the damage, they can affect the ability to absorb nutrients, reducing the plant population, which is reflected in lower yields.

Generally, areas with severe damage are very localized and it is common to find well-defined patches of infestation, where crops and weeds can be destroyed.

## Life cycle:

Most species of Phyllophaga lay their eggs in the soil, 2 to 10 cm deep; they are initially white, elongated, then become spherical. The larvae can complete their life cycle in one to two years.

The adults appear shortly after the first heavy rains of the season. They deposit eggs in the soil and the well-developed larvae are those that cause damage to the loroco plant roots. After 3 weeks they emerge, causing the destruction of the plant roots, affecting the ability to absorb nutrients, causing yellowing and stunting, which leads to the death of the plant (Figure 14).

Figure 13 Blind goat larva.

<!-- image -->

## Integrated management:

The control of large larvae is practically impossible, and often too late to try, therefore preventive measures must be taken before or at the time of planting.

To make the measures economically feasible, it is important to know which areas or fields are at risk of being attacked by the blind goat.

The difficulty of sampling small larvae makes forecasts more important in regions where annual species predominate. In areas where two-year cycle species predominate, sampling before planting is more effective, as in this case the larvae are large and easily visible.

## Biological control:

The larvae, being in a subterranean habitat, facilitate their control with the use of entomopathogenic fungi. The most widely used one is Metarhizium anisopliae, which upon contact with the insect, penetrates and feeds on it and reproduces, finally causing the death of the larvae. The entomopathogenic fungus is important to incorporate into the soil during land preparation, along with the seed; it can also be applied through drip irrigation.

Microbial control plays an important role in the management of Phyllophaga, especially for the small and medium farmer. This control, along with other methods, tends to reduce the amount of active ingredients applied.

Among the natural enemies, some parasitoids and predators are reported. In the hymenopteran order, Campsomeris dorsata (Scollidae family), Elis sp and Tiphia parallella (Tiphiidae) are mentioned. (Figure 15 and 16)

Figure 15 Blind goat predatory wasp.

<!-- image -->

Figure 16 Thiphiidae family wasp (Campsomeris dorsata).

<!-- image -->

Within the predators of Phyllophaga larvae, several Coleoptera and Diptera are included. In Coleoptera, the predators include adults and larvae of Carabidae and larvae of Elateridae. Larvae of some species of Asilidae and Tabanidae are also reported as Phyllophaga predators.

## Chemical control:

When the sampling indicates the presence of more than 4 large larvae per m2, Bifentrin 0.3 GR at a rate of 15 kg.ha -1 can be used along with fertilization at the time of planting. Irrational use can favor the resistance of species, increase the cost of cultivation, contaminate the environment, as well as cause problems of intoxication in users and above all reduce or eliminate the natural enemies of the blind goat, with which the potential of biological control is limited.

## Cultural control:

- Crop rotation (the most used).
- Soil preparation in advance of planting to allow biological controllers to act: chickens, birds, insects etc.
- Adults can be controlled with light lamps.
- Sowing between the last week of May and the first week of June.
- Soil covering that makes the environment unsuitable for the development of insects, because it reduces the acidity of the soil.

- Soil cultivation that allows the prepupas, pupae and adults to be exposed to the action of crop rotations, where grasses should be alternated with the sowing of legumes.

## APHID OR MITE ( Aphis sp.)

It is considered the most important pest that attacks the crop foliage.

Aphids are reproduced by winged or wingless females, without the need for male cells. They live in colonies that include all age groups, in the stems, petioles and buds of the leaves and flowers. They can be of great economic importance during the drought season (Figure 17).

For aphids, when conditions are adverse, they move to another plantation, if these are ideal, the aphids despite having wings do not migrate and continue to feed through their sucking mouthparts (Figure 18 and 19).

They are virtually cosmopolitan pests, with a soft body, they are frequently found in large numbers, sucking sap from the stems and leaves, which causes the weakening and death of the loroco.

Figure 16. Severe attack of aphids or mites on loroco flowers.

<!-- image -->

These insects are also reported as transmitters of viruses that curl the terminal, most tender leaves. In loroco foliage samples sent to the CIAT, it was determined through the ELISA test that two types of viruses transmitted by aphids existed.

The pest can also affect the clusters and flowers, and in addition, stop the growth of the stem, reducing the production of flowers.

Technical Guide

Aphids produce a melecilla that is expelled through the anus; it consists of an excess of sap to which sugars and other waste materials are added. When the melecilla is produced in excess, the surface of the objects that remain underneath becomes sticky and is the favorite food of many species of ants. This substance promotes the fumagina, which darkens the leaves; and it is more common under dry and hot conditions and when plants are subject to water stress (OIRSA, 2003).

Figure 18. Wingless aphid.

<!-- image -->

## Chemical control:

If there are colonies of aphids observed every ten during the dry season, if there is one aphid with wings per trap per day, insecticides such as imidaclopid 70 WG at a dose of 125 g.ha -1 at the time of sowing and 21 d.d.s; or Deltametrina 2.5 EC at a dose of 475 cc.ha -1 (286 cc.mz -1 ). When flowering is close, non-detergent soap can be used, at a rate of 4 grams per gallon of water or neem oil at a dose of 4 cc.l -1 of water. It is recommended to apply at least two applications per week to obtain good results.

## Biological control:

There is good biological control of aphids that attack the loroco, within the natural enemies we have:

Figure 20. Braconidae family wasp parasitizing an aphid.

<!-- image -->

Source: www.insectimages.org

Figure 21. Aphid in latency due to parasitoid.

<!-- image -->

Figure 19. Winged aphid.

<!-- image -->

- The parasitoid Lysiphebus testaceipes (Braconidae) that momifies the aphids and that can naturally maintain low aphid populations in the field (figure 20 and 21).
- Predators such as Ceratomegilla maculata, Cycloneda sanguínea and Hippodamia convergens, Scymnus sp. (Coccinellidae), Allograpta oblicua and other syrphidos (Diptera) (Navarro, C; García, F, 2018). Also entomopathogenic fungi such as Verticillium lecanii under conditions of humidity can cause high mortality in aphids.

<!-- image -->

Figure 22. Adult coccinellid.

Figure 23. Coccinellid larva.

<!-- image -->

Figure 24. Scymnus sp. larvae feeding on an aphid.

<!-- image -->

## TERMITES ( Atta sp and Acromyrmex sp)

These insects live in well-organized colonies of up to one million individuals, in large underground nests. The larvae and workers feed on the fungus (Rozites gonylophora) that they cultivate in the cut plant material brought to the nest by the workers.

## Damage:

The workers defoliate the loroco, mainly at night, attacking the seedlings, nurseries and established plantations in the field. They make semicircular cuts on the margins of the leaves, they can do this repeatedly and cause severe growth interruption.

## Control:

For termite control, baits are an alternative. Octaborate of Sodium 3% can be used at a dose of 2 kg.ha -1, this bait is placed around the loroco plants or near the termite nests. After the application of the bait, the colonies begin to recover the populations in 50 - 60 days.

Also, termites can be controlled by excavating the nests and applying water with soap or detergents to all termites that come out of the nest. This solution kills them instantly, as it covers their spiracles through which they breathe.

## FLOWER MITE (Diptera: Cecidomyiidae)

Figure 25. Larval stages of the flower mite.

<!-- image -->

Source: Arce, RA, Castellón ME; Martínez, JJ; 2007

The larvae of the flower mite are small and slender; in their development, they change color, from white, yellow to orange depending on age. These are pod-like, cylindrical with depressions and reduced on both sides, poorly developed head and tiny, modified mouthparts.

Flower mite larvae: (a, b, c and d) show color changes during their development (Figure 25).

<!-- image -->

## Damage:

The flower mite lays its eggs on the flower bud and penetrates the flower. Up to 60 larvae per bud can be found; damaged, forming galls on the flower, induced by the number of larvae inside the flower bud. It can be stated that as the gall increases in size and shape, a greater number of larvae are found inside it (Figure 26).

Its highest incidence and severity occur in July to November.

Figure 26. Flowers damaged by flower mite.

<!-- image -->

## Control:

The damage caused by the flower mite is usually minimal. It does not reduce yields and therefore, the economic losses are not significant for the producer. The damage found in the plantations does not justify the adoption of chemical control measures, as it does not affect the producer's profits, however, some control measures can be taken, such as the removal of damaged flowers.

## WHITE FLIE (Bemisia tabaci )

The white fly has become the main problem for many crops. It is a piercing-sucking insect, with the species Bemisia tabaci being the most widespread, distributed practically throughout the central and coastal zone of the country, at altitudes ranging from 0 to 800 msnm.

Its eggs remain on the underside of leaves for 5 to 10 days, and the nymphs for 12 to 28 days, these are translucent, yellow to yellow-green, passing through four stages from nymph to pupa. The nymphs suck sap from the underside of the leaves.

Technical Guide

The adult measures from 1 to 2 mm in length, white in color with two pairs of wings, is very active and agile (Figure 27 and 28), flying rapidly from its feeding sites, spreading viruses to healthy plants. The most important damage caused by these insects is the transmission of geminovirus (Figure 29).

<!-- image -->

Figure 27. Immature stage of white fly.

Figure 28. Adult white fly.

<!-- image -->

Figure 29. Tomato plant with severe virus damage transmitted by white fly.

<!-- image -->

## Integrated Management:

Cultural control can be carried out, using live barriers such as corn or sorghum. These should be planted around the planting area 40 days before transplanting, thus preventing the pest from reaching the early stages of the crop.

Another option for cultural control is the use of yellow traps with oil or glue, which should be placed at a height of 0.25 to 0.75 m, depending on the size of the crop, installed with the prevailing wind to attract a greater number of possible flies.

Control can be carried out with the application of 60 g of bread flour plus 30 g of powdered detergent mixed in 16 liters of water. The application controls eggs, nymphs and adults of white fly, when the presence of the insect is very high, it can be applied every 2 days until the presence of the pest decreases.

One of the critical points for carrying out chemical control is when 15 white flies are found per trap.

## Biological Control:

There are predators, parasitoids and entomopathogenic fungi of white fly such as: Eretmocerus mundus, Encarcia sp., Chrysopa sp., Coccinelids, Spiders and fungi such as Verticillium lecanii and Bauberia bassiana (Figure 30 and 31).

## Chemical Control:

Chemical applications should be carried out when 15 white flies are found.

Figure 30. Hymenopteran: Eretmocerus mundus parasitizing

<!-- image -->

white fly larvae. Source: www.bichelos.com

Figure 31. Encarcia sp parasitizing white fly eggs.

<!-- image -->

Source: University of Minnesota

per plant, recommending the use of insecticides with different active ingredients and in alternating form. Within the recommended insecticides are tiociclam -H-oxilate 50 SP, at a dose of 0.6 kg.ha -1 as a maximum 4 applications in the cycle at 10, 37, 65 and 86 d.d.s. Alternating Thiametoxan + Lambdacihalotrina 24.7 SC, at a dose of 0.5 lt.ha -1, 3 applications as a maximum in the cycle, at 15, 51 and 73 d.d.s.

## YELLOW MITES

## ( Polyphagotarsonemus latus )

It is called the tropical mite or yellow mite. It has a nearly universal distribution, with beans, tomatoes, peppers, cotton, coffee, citrus fruits, including loroco, as hosts.

Its eggs are pale white, oval, elongated, and have a series of tubercles on their surface, which are very typical of this species of mites.

The larvae are white, opaque, and their size is 0.15 mm long. They have three pairs of legs and have little mobility, moving slowly (Figure 32).

The adults are slightly yellowish, 0.2 mm long, their females are globose and oval. They multiply very quickly, can complete a generation in five days at 20ºC, prefer to live on the underside of leaves and usually proliferate in shaded and humid areas. They usually live on the tenderest leaves of the plants they attack.

## Damage:

The symptom it produces on the leaves is a diffuse, yellowish discoloration, desiccation, and even falling. In very severe attacks, it can cause the plant to wilt. When there are high populations of these mites, a tangle of silk invades the entire affected area.

Figure 32. State of mite nymph.

<!-- image -->

The invasion starts locally in foci from which it spreads, aided by the wind. If the number of mites on the plant is excessive, it migrates to the apical part of the leaves where it produces abundant silk that serves to disperse it.

## Integrated Management

## Biological Control:

The main natural enemies of mites belong to the group of predators. They can

<!-- image -->

<!-- image -->

exclusively feed on mites or have another alternative food source.

The most important insect orders of mite predators are: Coleoptera, Hemiptera (family Antocoridae and Miridae), Thysanoptera and Neuroptera (Chrysopa sp).

## Cultural Control:

There is a series of preventive measures that must be applied to control this pest such as:

- ·Placement of barriers against prevailing winds, as these areas are where mite attacks usually begin.
- ·Once the crop is finished and pruning is carried out, the stubble should be destroyed to prevent the spread of mites to new loroco sprouts.
- ·Remove weeds from the interior and exterior of the crop.

## Chemical Control:

The chemicals used to control mites are usually broad-spectrum pesticides that have an adverse effect on natural enemies, as well as on beneficial fauna, therefore chemical control should be within the framework of integrated control and should be used only when other forms of regulation of this pest are not effective.

Another important problem associated with the chemical control of mites is resistance to pesticides. In the tarsonemidae family, the egg stage is very resistant to acaricides, therefore the most appropriate ones should be chosen.

It is important to wet the sprouts and the underside of the leaves well, as this is where most of the population is located.

Acaricides such as Malation 57 EC at a dose of 1.4 to 2.8 l.ha -1 or Abamectin 18% at a dose of 0.5 l.ha -1 can be used.

## DISEASES

In El Salvador, the most frequent diseases that damage the crop are:

Technical Guide

## LEAF SPOT ( Cercospora sp.)

This pathogen produces a large number of lesions mainly during the last stages of the crop (Figure 33).

Figure 33. Cercospora march on loroco.

<!-- image -->

The fungus penetrates the plant through the stomata and develops mainly intracellularly. The optimal temperature for pathogen development is 25 to 28°C and high relative humidity. The fungus survives in crop residues and in some grasses such as Panicum sp; this grows very slowly on the plant, the symptoms appear around 30 days after the infection.

## Integrated Management:

The removal of alternative hosts and the incorporation of crop residues helps to reduce the incidence of the disease.

In places where the disease is severe, fungicides such as Oxichloride of copper (5.3 to 8 g.l -1 ) or Carbendazim (1.6 cc.l -1 of water) can be used.

Root rot ( Fusarium sp, Rhizoctonia sp): Root rot of loroco is caused by a complex of extremely destructive fungi and is considered as a conglomerate of diseases.

Plants damaged by these fungi show symptoms of drought, which results from the inability of the roots to supply water to the plant.

When extracting a plant with this disease, the root system is markedly reduced and is dark brown or black due to the presence of rot. Obviously, the damage to the roots prevents the absorption

and water and nutrient management, so that the foliage turns yellow and the leaves die.

## Integrated Management:

Use plants with healthy roots and avoid excessive moisture, perform light watering, sow in sites free of pathogens and apply Dazomet 98 GR to the soil, which acts as a soil fumigant. It is recommended for the control of nematodes, fungi and soil insects, annual and perennial weeds that come from seeds.

It is suggested to incorporate substrates rich in cellulose (bagasse, sorghum straw, etc.) so that other antagonistic organisms develop. Also, it is advisable to add lime to the soil.

The fungicides that can help reduce the damage are: Copper oxychloride 11.1% + Mancozeb 50% in doses of 2 to 3 kg.ha -1 , Copper hydroxide 53.8% in doses of 1 to 1.5 kg.ha -1 . The damaged plants must be removed, burying them or burning them to prevent their spread.

## LEAF SPOTS (Alternaria sp.)

Diseases caused by Alternaria sp. are very common in many types of plants around the world. The fungus affects leaves, stems, flowers, and fruits of annual plants; in general, diseases caused by Alternaria are known as leaf spots. The symptoms include leaf spots and leaf blight, seedling damping-off, stem and fruit rot.

## Damage:

The lesions of the diseases are present on the leaves in the form of circular brown spots, where concentric dark-colored rings are visible (Figure 34). Severely affected leaves change color from green to yellow, and easily detach from the plant (Figure 35).

Figure 34. Initial Alternaria sp. spot.

<!-- image -->

Figure 35. Advanced Alternaria sp. damage.

<!-- image -->

## Integrated Management:

To reduce the incidence of Alternaria, the seed must be treated with hot water, with fungicide and adequate fertilization. Fungicides that can be used are Chlorotalonil 72 SC in doses of 1.5 to 1.75 l.ha -1 , or Ditiocarbamate+ Mancozeb 80 WP in doses of 1.4 to 2.1 kg.ha -1 . The sprays should be initiated at the first symptoms of the affected plants and repeated every week; in plantations with a history of the disease, perform crop rotation, sanitary pruning, remove crop residues, and control weeds to prevent the incidence of inoculum for new plantings.

## XVI. IRRIGATION

Loroco is characterized by being a plant tolerant to relatively long periods of water stress.

The irrigation method will be selected considering technical, economic and social aspects; in other words, it will be necessary to analyze the amount and quality of the irrigation water, as well as the feasibility of investment and the attitude of the producer/farmer towards modern irrigation systems.

For example, in the case of straight furrows, it is recommended to use gravity irrigation, applying the water as close as possible to the area of influence of the crop roots.

Sprinkler irrigation is also used by many farmers in loroco plantations; however, with this method, the risk of contamination and disease spread is higher, so special attention must be paid to the quality of the water to reduce the incidence of these.

Another modality is drip irrigation. This is the most appropriate technical alternative when capital investment is available, because it allows for better use of water, fertilizer application, and better weed control by creating a moisture bulb in the root zone without mentioning the increase in crop productivity.

The film, frequency and time of irrigation must be calculated based on the type of irrigation, soil conditions, climate and plant growth stage in the production area.

<!-- image -->

## XVII. HARVEST AND POST-HARVEST

## HARVEST INDEX

Loroco flowers are highly perishable, they are harvested when they have reached their maximum development but before opening. These are characterized by the fact that the floral bud takes on a light green color or has a flower about to open.

The harvest is done every 2 or 3 days, because the development of the flowers is gradual. For this task, nylon mesh bags or rigid containers such as baskets and jugs are used, protected inside with blankets or with musa leaves to prevent physical damage, such as abrasions that will translate into quality losses due to the darkening (oxidation) of the flowers.

The container for harvesting must be completely clean, and the harvesters must practice good hygiene habits.

It is recommended to harvest in the early morning, protecting the product from the sun to avoid loss of moisture and quality.

## SELECTION AND CLEANING

Selection consists of removing the open flowers, as these deteriorate quickly, creating excess moisture that causes the decomposition of the rest of the flowers. Also, the flowers damaged by insects, leaves and any other foreign material are removed. This practice is very common among producers with larger plots, as it helps to achieve a better presentation, longer conservation and good prices.

## TRANSPORT

Loroco, being a perishable product, must be transported in conditions of controlled temperature and relative humidity to avoid weight loss and wilting.

In the country, these conditions are not available for small producers, who use open transport. In this case, it is recommended to protect the flowers

Technical Guide

with blankets or musa leaves inside the baskets or jugs, do not overfill the containers, avoid excessive stacking to prevent compaction of the product and transport it in the early morning to its final destination.

It is important to consider the distances to the markets of the product, to define the most convenient harvest time. Some producers in more distant areas harvest in the early morning (4 pm or later) and transport the product in the first hours of the next day.

This system has the advantage that the harvested flower is exposed to the environment, which has a lower temperature and higher relative humidity, conditions that favor its conservation. During the night, the product is stored spread on flat and clean surfaces, in layers of 8 to 10 cm in height. When the market is close, it should be harvested in the morning in the early hours (5 am or later).

## XVIII. STORAGE AND MARKETING

Loroco is preferred fresh in local and international markets, because in this state it conserves its organoleptic characteristics. Due to its perishability, the most used conservation methods are: refrigeration and freezing, with chemical and natural preservatives and dehydration.

In Figure 36, the exports of loroco that have occurred in El Salvador between 2009 and 2013 are shown. El Salvador has signed free trade agreements with Mexico, the Dominican Republic, Chile, Panama, Spain, the United States and Colombia. All are currently in force.

Figure 36. Export of loroco in El Salvador, 2009-2013. Source: Central Bank of El Salvador.

<!-- image -->

## REFRIGERATION AND FREEZING

Through domestic refrigeration, it is possible to preserve loroco for a period of 8 days, the quality achieved will depend on the handling conditions to which it is subjected, prior to cooling. The product is selected, washed with a 50 ppm chlorine solution, drained and packaged in 1-pound plastic bags, placed in stacks of up to 3 bags to avoid damage caused by weight. Storage in a cold room, with controlled temperature and humidity, allows to extend the conservation time. Loroco for export is preserved by rapid freezing, and even under these conditions, there are losses of aroma and quality deterioration; however, it allows to preserve it in better conditions and for a longer time, than in domestic freezers, in which it has been possible to preserve it in acceptable conditions for up to three months.

The loss of quality in frozen loroco is mainly associated with the loss of aroma. The peculiar smell of loroco is composed of several very volatile compounds. A better understanding of these would allow to improve the storage handling practices.

## PRESERVATION

This consists in preserving the loroco in solutions based on sodium chloride, natural vinegar, oil and acetic acid.

- Loroco in brine: this consists in preserving the loroco in a solution of sodium chloride (Na Cl) at concentrations that can vary from 0.25% to 1.0% in relation to weight/volume.
- Pickled loroco: this method is artisanal and consists in mixing the loroco with other types of vegetables and flavoring spices and pickling them with natural vinegar or with chemical products such as acetic acid.
- Loroco in oil: the loroco can be mixed with edible oils such as olive, sunflower, sesame and corn, and offered to gourmet markets. It can be packaged in plastic, glass or plastic bags and in cans.

## DEHYDRATION

Dehydrated loroco: this consists in extracting all the moisture from the product, exposing it to the sun with special equipment. It is used in soups, instant creams or tea.

## QUALITY STANDARDS

The experiences of commercial transactions abroad allow to deduce that each buyer defines its own quality standards depending on its demand. Some prefer the floral bunch with closed flowers and with certain uniformity in size; for this, it is necessary to remove the smaller flowers, a task that is carried out with razor blades.

Others require that the peduncle of the flower has a length of 1 cm. In general, the external market requires that the loroco is free of chemicals, in this case, it is recommended to use oxygenated water instead of chlorine for washing the flower. For the local consumer, the product is preferred fresh without oxidation spots, without open flowers and without damage from insects.
